Abstract written by a model

In Hesiod, the Homeric Hymns, and Homerica, Heracles is the mightiest of men who performs arduous deeds for Eurystheus, fights monsters and enemies, and becomes immortal on Olympus with Hebe. In The Extant Odes of Pindar, Herakles is a mighty son of Zeus and Alkmene who performs labours, fights enemies, establishes Olympia and its games, and dwells peacefully among the gods. In The Seven Plays in English Verse, Heracles is an exiled hero whose conquest of Oechalia, love for Iole, and poisoned robe lead to his death on Mount Oeta and his appearance as a divine messenger. In Alcestis, Heracles is a demi-god who, after learning that Alcestis has died, fights Death and returns her alive to Admetus. In The Tragedies of Euripides, Volume I, Hercules is a laboring hero who restores Alcestis from death, fathers children, dies by fire, and undergoes apotheosis with Hebe. In Hecuba and other plays, Hercules is a strong benefactor of Greece whose labours and rescue of Theseus are followed by madness, the killing of his family, exile, and eventual dwelling among the gods. In The Argonautica, Heracles is a powerful Argonaut who defers leadership to Jason, fights and travels with the crew, searches for Hylas, and is left behind at Mysia. In The Historical Library of Diodorus the Sicilian, in Fifteen Books, Hercules is a powerful civilizing conqueror who performs labours, defeats enemies, founds settlements, aids allies, and is translated to the gods.

and 11 more works

In The Aeneid, Hercules is Jupiter’s warrior-hero son who kills Geryon and Cacus, drives cattle to the Tiber, fathers Aventinus, and is invoked by Pallas before combat. In Heroides and Amores, Hercules is a laboring hero and husband of Deianira who conquers Oechalia, serves Omphale, submits to Iole, and is reported to be dying from a poisoned cloak. In The Library, Hercules is a hero who performs the labours imposed by Eurystheus, battles monsters and enemies, rescues Theseus and Alcestis, and gains immortality after his death. In The Metamorphoses of Ovid, Books I-VII, Hercules is the Tirynthian hero who is suckled by Juno, slays Hydra, captures Cos, brings Cerberus from Tartarus, and dies on Oeta. In The Metamorphoses of Ovid, Books VIII-XV, Hercules is a heroic protector who performs great labours, defeats monsters and tyrants, conquers Troy, suffers from Nessus’s poisoned tunic, and is received into heaven. In The Library, Hercules is a mortal hero who performs labours for Eurystheus, aids gods and heroes, campaigns against enemies, and becomes immortal after dying on a pyre. In Description of Greece, Hercules is a Greek hero and later a god whose labours, battles, cults, temples, games, and artistic representations are associated with sites across Greece. In Pausanias' Description of Greece, Volume II, Hercules is chiefly presented through local traditions, monuments, temples, statues, and conflicting accounts of his deeds and identity. In The Fall of Troy, Hercules is a mighty hero who fathers Telephus and becomes the grandfather of Eurypylus, with his strength and shield honoring him. In Bulfinch's Mythology: The Age of Fable, Hercules is a hero who performs the twelve Labors, serves Omphale, marries Dejanira, dies from Nessus’s poison, and becomes immortal among the stars. In An Astronomer's Wife: Heracles, presented in Angeline Hall’s poem as the genius of labor, performs courageous, enduring tasks to please God, bless humanity, and practice virtue: he rescues Hesione, overcomes Death, receives Helios’s golden boat, and models demanding work.

One sentence for each work Hercules is in, in date order. A model wrote them when it joined the works; each title opens that work’s own account below.

written by a model marks a model’s summary or wording. the source’s own words are the quotations: each one opens the source text at its passage, where it can be read in context and judged.

In each work

Hercules in each of the 19 accounts the graph joined, in date order. Nothing is merged: an abstract, a narrative and a claim stay with the work they were written from.

Heracles in Hesiod, the Homeric Hymns, and Homerica

Hesiod · about 699 BC · the work’s page · read it where Heracles first appears

Abstract written by a model

Heracles is a man on earth and the mightiest of men, born in Thebes as the son of Zeus and Alcmena, also called the son of Amphitryon and the great-hearted offspring of Alcaeus. He is twin brother to Iphiclus, though they differ in parentage and spirit. Described as mighty, valiant, bold-hearted, lion-hearted, most toilful, and most excellent, he is marked by extraordinary strength, violence, hardship, and eventual divine status. He undertakes many deeds and wanderings over land and sea at Eurystheus’s bidding, equipped with a lion-skin, long spear, and great shield, including a shield made by Hephaestus. He travels with Iolaus, serves as his charioteer in the war against Cycnus, kills Cycnus, and then fights and wounds Ares. He defeats Centaurs, releases Prometheus by killing the eagle that consumes his liver, attacks and sacks Pylos after Neleus refuses to purify him for killing Iphitus, and sacks Oechalia for Iole. He also travels across the sea by cauldron, is left behind in Magnesia near Aphetae, repeatedly visits Ceyx’s house at Trachis, and is entertained by Telamon while praying in his lion-skin. He is associated with Black-bottom. After completing grievous labours, he becomes immortal and unageing among the gods. He lives happily in snowy Olympus, where he marries Hebe. He is also said to have loved Deianeira, with whom he had Hyllus, Glenus, Ctesippus, and Odites, and he is identified as Hyllus’s father.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

11 sections; each heading opens the text

The Hesiodic Poems

Heracles attacks and sacks Pylos after Neleus refuses to purify him for the murder of Iphitus, killing several of Neleus’s sons, including Periclymenus. He is also associated with the Shield of Heracles, the Aegimius, the Marriage of Ceyx, and other episodes that may have developed from the Catalogues of Women.

(ll. 453-491) But Rhea was subject in love to Cronos and bare splendid

Heracles, the son of Alcmene, kills the eagle that eats Prometheus's liver and releases Prometheus from his affliction. Zeus allows this so that Heracles's glory may become greater.

(ll. 881-885) But when the blessed gods had finished their toil, and

Heracles was born to Zeus and Alcmena and was called mighty and valiant. After completing his grievous labours, he married Hebe in Olympus and became deathless and unageing among the gods.

Fragment #67—Scholiast on Euripides, Orestes 249: Steischorus says that

Heracles is called the great-hearted offspring of Alcaeus and is the only figure named as daring to fight a formidable warrior with the spear. He loved Deianeira, who bore him Hyllus, Glenus, Ctesippus, and Odites, and he encountered Auge while seeking Laomedon’s horses and fighting the Amazons.

THE SHIELD OF HERACLES

Heracles is the stronger and more formidable son of Alcmena, born from her union with Zeus. He arms himself with divine equipment, including a breastplate from Athena and a shield made by Hephaestus, then goes with Iolaus to fight Cycnus and Ares.

(ll. 402-412) As two lions 1806 on either side of a slain deer spring

Heracles strikes Cycnus in the neck beneath the chin and kills him. He then faces Ares and wounds him in the exposed thigh before going with Iolaus to Trachis.

THE MARRIAGE OF CEYX

Heracles landed from the Argo to look for water and was left behind in Magnesia near Aphetae because of his desertion there. He is also represented as repeatedly visiting Ceyx’s house at Trachis and speaking about the good attending the feasts of the good.

THE GREAT EOIAE

Heracles is described as the son of Zeus and Alcmene, and as destined to be the most toilful and the most excellent. He is also identified as the father of Hyllus and is said to have been entertained by Telamon while wearing his lion-skin and praying.

XV. TO HERACLES THE LION-HEARTED

Heracles is the son of Zeus and Alcmena and is called the mightiest of men on earth. He performed many deeds of violence and endured many hardships while wandering over land and sea at Eurystheus's bidding. He now lives happily in Olympus and is married to Hebe.

THE TAKING OF OECHALIA

Heracles sacked Oechalia for the sake of Eurytus's daughter Iole.

THE CERCOPES

Heracles was identified with Black-bottom, whom the mother of the Cercopes warned them to avoid.

Herakles in The Extant Odes of Pindar

Pindar · 497 BC · the work’s page · read it where Herakles first appears

Abstract written by a model

Herakles is a mighty son of Zeus and Alkmene, also linked with Amphitryon through Zeus’s visit in Amphitryon’s likeness. He is a hero of divinely granted valour and wisdom, whose deeds are continually celebrated, and later a god honored by the immortals. As an infant, he was the target of serpents sent by Hera and strangled them. Teiresias foretold that he would overcome monsters and enemies, fight the giants beside the gods, gain rest from his labors, and marry Hebe. Much of Herakles’ course is defined by great and numerous labors and service to Eurystheus under compulsion. He travels across earth and sea, makes passage safe for ships, sets the Pillars of Herakles as the end of voyaging, wrestles Antaios in Libya, fights giants, and kills Alkyoneus. In war he leads Tirynthian men, joins Telamon against Troy, destroys Troy and Pergamos, and kills the Meropes. At Pylos he contends with Poseidon and is opposed by Poseidon, Phoibos, and Hades. His most enduring foundation is at Pisa and Olympia. After defeating Kleatos and Eurytos and forcing Augeas to pay for service, he gathers host and booty, establishes the Altis and its boundaries, honors Alpheos and the twelve greatest gods, sanctifies Zeus’s altars, and founds the Olympic games. He establishes the fifth-year feast, brings the olive tree from Ister, gives the Dioskouroi charge of the spectacle, and names the Hill of Kronos. After his toils, Herakles marries Hebe and dwells in Olympus, beside Zeus, in peace and divine rest. He remains a recipient of sacrifices and games at Thebes, a friend of Telamon and Aiakos, and a source of strength for mortals facing difficulties.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

10 sections; each heading opens the text

III.

Herakles fulfilled the behests of old by establishing the games and brought the olive tree from the land of Ister to Zeus's precinct. He also received help from Leto's horse-loving daughter during his quest for the golden-horned hind.

IX.

At Pylos, Herakles wielded his club against Poseidon, Phoibos, and Hades, who opposed him with a trident, a silver bow, and a staff. The ode then warns against speaking evil of the gods.

XI.

Herakles founded the Olympic games after killing Kleatos and Eurytos and forcing Augeas to pay for service done. He gathered his host and booty at Pisa, established the Altis and its boundaries, honored the Alpheos and the twelve greatest gods, and instituted the fifth-year feast.

IX.

Herakles is praised as a figure whom no one should fail to celebrate. The speaker credits Herakles and Iphikles, together with the waters of Dirke that nurtured them, in connection with his own successful song.

Nemean Ode I

Herakles is celebrated for his ancient deeds of valor. As an infant he strangled two serpents sent against him, and Teiresias prophesied that he would destroy many monsters, defeat enemies, fight beside the gods against the giants, receive rest from his labors, and marry Hebe.

Nemean Ode IV

Herakles is associated with a splendid sepulchre at Thebes and with a fortunate hall where Timasarchos stayed as a friend. Together with Telamon, he destroyed Troy, the Meropes, and the warrior Alkyoneus.

Nemean Ode VII

Herakles is called a loving friend and brother of Aiakos and is invoked alongside Zeus and Athene. Sogenes is described as wishing to live happily in the sacred street of his ancestors in a close bond with Herakles, whose favor can give mortals strength against difficulties.

Nemean Ode X

Herakles was born from Zeus's union with Alkmene after Zeus entered Amphitryon's hall in Amphitryon's likeness. Herakles became a bridegroom of Hebe in Olympos, and his ordinance at Pisa is identified with the Olympic games.

Isthmian Ode III

Herakles is praised as the son of Alkmene who traveled across the earth and sea, made safe passage for ships, and overcame Antaios. He now dwells beside the aegis-bearer, is honored by the immortals, has Hebe as his wife, and receives sacrifices and games at Thebes.

Isthmian Ode V

Herakles took Telamon and the Tirynthians by ship to Troy to avenge Laomedon’s falsehood. He captured Pergamos, killed the Meropes and the giant herdsman Alkyoneus, prayed for Telamon’s son, and received Zeus’s sign through the eagle.

Heracles in The Seven Plays in English Verse

Sophocles · 449 to 429 BC · the work’s page · read it where Heracles first appears

Abstract written by a model

Heracles is the son of Zeus and Alcmena, a world-champion and divine hero, husband of Deianira, father of Hyllus, and later a deified hero speaking from a heavenly seat. He lives in exile at Trachis with Deianira, though she says he habitually takes other women as lovers. He has also been enslaved for a year to Omphalè in Lydia and is associated with an exceptional bow later owned by Philoctetes. After fifteen months campaigning in Euboea, he conquers and sacks Oechalia, his last exploit. The records stress that his love for Iole, whom he sends ahead with Lichas to Trachis, drives this conquest: he is pierced and overmastered by the maiden's love. At Cenaeum, while sacrificing to Zeus, he puts on Deianira's robe. It adheres to him and produces unbearable internal pain. In agony he kills the guiltless Lichas and has Hyllus carry him away. Learning that Deianira acted through Nessus's deception and has died, he understands prophecies as marking his death. He orders Hyllus to burn him on Mount Oeta and to marry Iole. At the conclusion he appears from the sky as a divine messenger, gives prophecy to Philoctetes and Neoptolemus, promises Philoctetes healing, glory, and victory at Troy, and tells them to protect one another and honor the gods.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

5 sections; each heading opens the text

THE TRACHINIAN MAIDENS

Heracles has been absent from Trachis for fifteen months while campaigning in Euboea. He has conquered Oechalia, taken its women captive, and sent Iole ahead to his household. According to the Messenger, he destroyed Oechalia because he loved Iole.

DÊ. Nay, I entreat thee by His name, whose fire

Heracles is said to have been pierced by the maiden's love and wholly overmastered by it. He conquered Oechalia because of her love, and he did not command Lichas to conceal or deny the reason.

CHORUS.

Heracles returns after sacking Eurytus' city and sends the captive maiden to his household. At Cenaeum, he wears Deianira's robe while sacrificing to Zeus, but the garment clings to his body and causes violent internal pain. He blames the gift, kills the guiltless Lichas in agony, and orders Hyllus to carry him away from the place.

CHORUS.

Heracles is brought in on a litter, suffering unbearable pain from the robe sent by Deianira. After learning that Deianira has died and acted under Nessus's deception, he interprets the prophecies as foretelling his death and commands Hyllus to burn him on Mount Oeta. He also orders Hyllus to marry Iole.

MONODY.

Heracles appears from above as a divine messenger and tells Philoctetes and Neoptolemus what Zeus intends. He promises Philoctetes healing, glory, and victory at Troy, and instructs the two men to protect one another and honor the gods.

Heracles in Alcestis

Euripides · 437 BC · the work’s page · read it where Heracles first appears

Abstract written by a model

Heracles is a demi-god, Zeus-born son of Alcmena, a hero and figure of the Satyr world: boisterous, convivial, and especially suited to Satyr-play, yet also a tragic deliverer. Traveling through Pherae on the Argive King’s command to seize Diomede’s man-eating horses in Thrace, he arrives tired and is welcomed as Admetus’s guest. Unaware that Alcestis has died, he feasts, drinks, and sings amid the household’s concealed grief. Once a Servant tells him the truth, his revelling gives way to horror and resolve. To repay Admetus’s hospitality and as his friend, he goes among the dead, fights and wrestles Death, and returns Alcestis alive. Back at Admetus’s house, he first presents her as a woman won in contests, then reveals her identity, and departs to continue his quest rather than stay for a feast. He is portrayed as triumphant, kind, wise, and still marked by a mocking, Satyric spirit.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

4 sections; each heading opens the text

INTRODUCTION

Heracles is presented as a heroic reveller who arrives tired, feasts, drinks, and sings before learning of Alcestis’s death. He then becomes sober, goes out to battle Death, wrestles with him, and brings Alcestis home; he remains triumphant, kindly, and wise while still retaining something of the mocking and boisterous Satyric atmosphere.

ALCESTIS

Apollo says that Heracles is coming at the command of a southland king to fetch four wild horses. Apollo foretells that Heracles will wrest Alcestis from Thanatos and the dead.

[_As the song ceases there enters a stranger, walking strongly, but

Heracles comes to Pherae while traveling to Thrace to seize Diomede's man-eating horses for the Argive King. Admetus welcomes him despite the funeral in the household, and Heracles feasts without knowing that Alcestis is the dead woman. When the Servant reveals the truth, Heracles is horrified and sets out to rescue Alcestis from Death.

[_The stage is empty; then_ ADMETUS _and the_ CHORUS

Heracles returns after winning a woman in contests and asks Admetus to keep her in his house. He reveals that she is Alcestis, whom he recovered by fighting Death, then leaves to continue his task rather than remain for the feast.

Hercules in The Tragedies of Euripides, Volume I

Euripides · 437 BC · the work’s page · read it where Hercules first appears

Abstract written by a model

Hercules is a hero, son of Alcmena and of Zeus, Jove, or Jupiter, and of the blood of Perseus. Sent by and serving Eurystheus, he performs imposed labors, including killing Hydra at Lerna and seeking the chariot-horses of Diomed in Thrace. While on that labor, he is welcomed by Admetus despite Admetus's grief. At first unaware that Alcestis has died, Hercules feasts; after learning the truth, he opposes Death, resolves to seize Death or descend to the underworld, and restores Alcestis from the tomb to Admetus's house. He is also father of the children, Macaria, and the sons of Hercules, and is recalled as a companion of Iolaus whom Theseus brought out of the underworld. Feared and persecuted by Eurystheus, he dies, his body is consumed in fire, and he undergoes apotheosis, entering heaven and joining Hebe.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

6 sections; each heading opens the text

ALCESTIS.

Apollo predicts that Hercules, sent by Eurystheus to bring back a chariot and horses from Thrace, will be welcomed at Admetus's house and will take Alcestis away from Death by force.

CHOR. And I indeed with thee, as a friend with a friend, will bear this

Hercules arrives seeking Admetus while undertaking a labor for Eurystheus: capturing the chariot-horses of Diomed, son of Mars. Admetus receives him as a guest despite the funeral, and Hercules does not initially know that Alcestis has died.

CHOR. Desist, for the present calamity is sufficient; and do not, O son,

Hercules initially feasts and drinks in the house of Admetus without knowing that Alcestis has died. When the servant reveals the truth, Hercules resolves to seize Death or descend to the underworld and bring Alcestis back.

THE HERACLIDÆ.

Hercules is described as the father of the suppliants, the son of Jupiter and Alcmena, and a former companion of Iolaus. Theseus once aided Hercules by bringing him out of the underworld, creating an obligation that Iolaus invokes.

CHOR. This land is ever willing to aid in a just cause those in difficulty;

Hercules is identified as the father of the children and of Macaria. Macaria invokes his noble descent when presenting her willingness to die, and Iolaus praises her as worthy of Hercules's divine-minded lineage.

SERV. O mistress, I bring news most concise for you to hear, and to myself

Hercules is described as a powerful and worthy man whom Eurystheus feared and persecuted. The Chorus says that Hercules has gone to heaven, his body consumed in fire, and that he now shares the bed of Hebe.

Hercules in Hecuba and other plays

Euripides · about 429 BC · the work’s page · read it where Hercules first appears

Abstract written by a model

Hercules is a hero of extraordinary strength, a benefactor of Greece, and a figure defined by arduous service, family catastrophe, endurance, and eventual dwelling among the gods. He is identified as the son of Jove and also of Amphitryon, through Alcmena; he is Megara’s husband and the father of their children, with Macaria also counted among his children. He has a house in Thebes and is honored there by an altar of Jupiter for victory over the Minyans. For Eurystheus, Hercules performs labours intended to rid the world of pests. His deeds include slaying the Nemean lion and wearing its hide, subduing the Lernaean Hydra, killing Geryon, Cycnus, and the dragon of the Hesperian gardens, capturing the Ceryneian hind, mastering Diomedes’ man-eating horses and bringing them to Eurystheus, battling the Amazons while seeking Hippolita’s belt, relieving Atlas by carrying the heavens’ load, and wounding Centaurs. His final labour takes him into the Underworld, where he defeats and brings back Cerberus and rescues Theseus. While he is absent, Megara, Amphitryon, and his children are threatened by Lycus. Hercules returns, learns of the danger, and kills Lycus. Soon afterward, however, divine hostility sends him into madness. Mistaking his own family for Eurystheus’ children, he kills his children and Megara with his weapons. Athena stops the frenzy, and, when he wakes, he is bound, polluted by the murders, overwhelmed by grief and shame, and considers suicide. He cannot bury those he killed and must leave Thebes under its laws of pollution. Theseus persuades him to endure rather than die. Hercules agrees to live, keep his weapons, and depart after Amphitryon performs the burial. Later records remember him as dead but dwelling in heaven, restored among the gods and married to Hebe. His children continue to suffer exile and persecution because of their connection to him, while his courage, labours, rescue of Theseus, and service to Greece remain central to his memory.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

9 sections; each heading opens the text

HERCULES DISTRACTED.

Hercules is described as the son of Jove and Amphitryon and the husband of Megara. He performed many labours, including killing the Hydra and the Nemean lion, but has descended to the Underworld and has not returned. Lycus threatens his family, while Amphitryon and the Chorus defend his courage and his services to Thebes and Greece.

CHORUS.

Hercules is praised for his matchless fortitude and for completing many labours. He slew numerous monsters, captured Diomedes’ horses, defeated the Amazons, and entered the underworld.

MEGARA, AMPHITRYON, CHORUS.

Hercules is described as deceased and absent while his father, children, and Megara face death. Megara calls on him to hear them from Pluto’s realm and appear, even as a ghost, because his presence would be enough to protect his children.

HERCULES, MEGARA, AMPHITRYON, CHORUS.

Hercules returns safely from the Underworld and is astonished to find Megara, his children, and Amphitryon in funeral garments. After learning that Lycus intends to kill them, he hides in the palace, vows to avenge his family, and kills Lycus.

IRIS, A FIEND, CHORUS.

Hercules is driven mad by the Fiend and mistakes his own children for the children of Eurystheus, killing them and Megara with his weapons. Athena stops him by striking him with a rocky fragment, after which he is bound to a pillar; on waking, he learns of the murders and is overcome by grief, shame, and thoughts of suicide.

THESEUS, AMPHITRYON, HERCULES, CHORUS.

Hercules is found mourning and ashamed after killing his children and wife during a frenzy. He believes that his sufferings, pollution, and expected exile make life impossible, but Theseus persuades him to endure and accompany him to Athens. Hercules agrees to live, retain his weapons, and leave Thebes after Amphitryon performs the burial.

THE CHILDREN OF HERCULES.

Hercules is dead and is said to dwell in heaven. Iolaus formerly shared his labors, while his children now suffer exile and persecution because of their connection to him. Hercules is also remembered as having rescued Theseus from the caves of Pluto.

DEMOPHOON, IOLAUS, CHORUS.

Hercules is identified as the father of the children and the companion whose memory makes them targets of Eurystheus. Macaria invokes his name with pride and says that her conduct must be worthy of the father from whom she descends. Iolaus recalls fighting beside Hercules when he laid waste to the Spartan realms.

SERVANT, ALCMENA, CHORUS.

Hercules is said to dwell with the gods and to have appeared with Hebe as one of two stars above Iolaus's chariot. He is praised for his courage and deeds, while Eurystheus is accused of sending him into deadly labors and of persecuting his family.

Heracles in The Argonautica

Apollonius Rhodius · 299 to 200 BC · the work’s page · read it where Heracles first appears

Abstract written by a model

Heracles is a hero of mighty heart and strength, a son of Zeus, and is regarded as the bravest candidate for leadership. He is Hylas’s young companion’s protector: he has nurtured Hylas from childhood, while Hylas bears his arrows and guards his bow. When Jason summons the heroes, Heracles comes from Arcadia by way of Lyrceian Argos. He refuses leadership in Jason’s favor, shares Argo’s middle bench with Ancaeus, helps launch the ship, and uses his club in sacrifice. He presses the Argonauts to leave the Lemnian women and resume the fleece quest. In later fighting and travel, Heracles kills Earthborn attackers, Telecles, and Megabrontes, rows with exceptional force, and breaks an oar near the Mysian mainland. At Cius he enters the woods to cut a pine for a replacement oar. Learning from Polyphemus that Hylas is missing, he abandons the pine and searches frantically, threatening the Mysians with destruction unless Hylas’s fate is found. He is left behind there, against the Argonauts’ will; Lycus mourns the loss of his aid and recalls his defeat of Titias and subjugation of neighboring peoples. His destined course is to return to Argos, labor for Eurystheus, and afterward dwell with the immortals. His deeds include killing Theiodamas, driving birds from the Stymphalian lake with a bronze rattle, killing Ladon with hydra-gall arrows to take the golden apples, and making a spring near the Tritonian lake by striking rock with his foot. He acts against Eurystheus’s purpose and is the only hero able to withstand Aeetes’ spear.

Hercules in The Historical Library of Diodorus the Sicilian, in Fifteen Books

Diodorus Siculus · 59 to 29 BC · the work’s page · read it where Hercules first appears

Abstract written by a model

Hercules is presented in several overlapping traditions, chiefly as the extraordinarily strong hero and son of Jupiter and Alcmena, but also as an ancient Egyptian, Indian, or elder son of Jupiter distinct from Alceus, the later Hercules. Across these accounts he is a civilizing conqueror: he frees lands from wild beasts, giants, tyrants, bondage, and hostile peoples; founds or builds cities and colonies; opens routes; aids allies; and receives worship and sacrifice. After education in Thebes, strength and virtue distinguish him, but Juno inflicts madness in which he kills the children he had with Megara. Ordered by Eurystheus, he performs the labours, including defeating beasts and enemies, cleansing Augeas's stable, rescuing Prometheus, fighting Amazons, taking Gerion's oxen, entering hell for Cerberus, and obtaining the Hesperides' treasure. His travels range through Africa, Iberia, Celtica, Italy, Sicily, Egypt, Ethiopia, Sardinia, and Peloponnesus, where he destroys opponents, founds or improves places, and establishes the Olympic games. He joins and is chosen general of the Argonauts, takes Troy after killing Laomedon, and gives its kingdom to Priam. Later he marries Dejanira, accidentally kills Eurynonius, is exiled, fights further wars, and dies on Mount Oeta after receiving her poisoned garment. He is then translated to the gods, worshipped by gods and people, adopted by Juno, and granted immortality. He is remembered as father of the Heraclidae and Tlepolemus, and as a figure whose deeds and name are sometimes assigned to distinct earlier Hercules figures.

We shall first speak of the barbarians; not that we judge them

Hercules, described as Osiris's near kinsman, was left as general of all the forces within Osiris's dominions. He was admired for his courage and bodily strength.

From Osiris and Isis, to the reign of Alexander the Great, who

The Egyptians distinguish an ancient Hercules, said to be the son of Jupiter, from the later Hercules who was the son of Alcmena and was initially called Alceus. The ancient Hercules is credited with fighting giants, freeing the earth from wild beasts, civilizing lands, and setting up a pillar in Africa, while the later Hercules is said to have adopted the name in rivalry with him.

India is of a quadrangular form, one side lying towards the east^

The Indians say that Hercules was born among them and surpassed other men in strength and courage. He divided India among his sons and daughter, built many cities including Palibothra, and was honored as a god after his death.

Hercules, the son of Jupiter and Alcmena, was ordered by Eurystheus to fight Hippolyte and take her belt. He defeated the Amazons in battle and captured Hippolyte and her belt.

CHAP. IV.

Hercules extirpated both the Gorgons and the Amazons when he traveled into the western parts and erected a pillar in Africa. He could not tolerate a nation being governed by women because he sought renown throughout the world.

DIODORUS SICULUS- / BOOK IV.

Hercules is introduced as a hero whose actions exceeded those of all others recorded in the world. Diodorus says that his deeds are difficult to relate because their greatness and strangeness may seem incredible, but he will report them according to ancient poets and mythologists.

Perseus (they say) was the son of Jupiter by Danae, the daughter

Hercules is presented as exceptionally strong and virtuous from birth. He strangles two serpents sent by Juno, frees Thebes from Erginus, marries Megara, kills his children during a fit of madness, and then undertakes the labours imposed by Eurystheus. He performs numerous feats, including killing the Nemean lion and Hydra, defeating the centaurs, assisting the gods against the giants, freeing Prometheus, and defeating the Amazons.

After this, a tenth labour was imposed upon him by Em7stheii%

Hercules undertook the tenth labour imposed by Eurystheus and assembled a fleet and army for the expedition to Iberia. He defeated the sons of Chrysaor, took the oxen of Gerion, and travelled through Libya, Iberia, Gaul, Italy, and Sicily, where he destroyed wild beasts and tyrants, improved lands and roads, performed various works, and received honours and sacrifices.

Having finished his tenth labour, £urystheus imposed anoAer

Hercules was initiated at Eleusis before entering hell, where he freed Theseus and Pirithous and brought Cerberus away. He obtained the golden apples or sheep of the Hesperides, founded a colony in Sardinia through Iolaus and the Thespiadae, served Omphale, captured Troy, and fought Augeas and Hippocoon. He later became involved with Auge and married Deianira after Meleager's death.

Hercules diverted the river Achelous to benefit the Calydonians and assisted them against the Thesproti. He later fought several wars, killed Eurynonius accidentally, received the poisoned garment from Dejanira, died on Mount Oeta, and was believed to have been translated among the gods.

CHAR m.

Hercules is chosen as general of the Argonauts because of his exceptional valor. During the voyage he frees Hesione and kills the sea monster threatening Troy, later rescues the sons of Phineus and kills Phineus and many Thracians, and receives prophecies from Glaucus about his labors and future immortality.

Hercules proposed that the Argonauts swear to assist one another and left him to establish games at a notable place in Greece. He chose the grounds near the Alpheus in Elis, instituted the Olympic Games, and later became widely celebrated for his deeds.

Hercules was the father of the Heraclidae and Tlepolemus, and his brother's son was Iolaus. The kingdom entrusted to Aegimius by Hercules was later restored to the Heraclidae.

,CHAP. IV.

Hercules is presented as a model whom Theseus imitated in his brave and noble actions. He had brought the Marathonian bull from Crete into Peloponnesus as one of his labours. He later secured Theseus’s release from the shades below.

Hercules is said to have sent children born by the daughters of Thespius, with Iolaus and a large mixed company, to establish settlements in Sardinia. He also instituted celebrated games, and the people of the Gymnesian islands explain their prohibition of silver and gold by recalling his war against Geryon.

Hercules marched into Celtica while engaged in war against Gallia and built Alesia there. He fathered Galates with the daughter of the ruler of Celtica.

The unit distinguishes an elder Hercules, said to be the son of Jupiter, from the later Hercules who was the son of Alcmena. The elder Hercules traveled through the whole world, punished the wicked, destroyed wild beasts, delivered people from bondage, and remained unconquered and invulnerable. The later Hercules was credited with the elder Hercules's deeds because they shared the same name.

Hercules in The Aeneid

Virgil · 28 to 18 BC · the work’s page · read it where Hercules first appears

Abstract written by a model

Hercules is a god, Jupiter’s warrior-hero son and Aventinus’s father. After slaying the triple Geryon, he drives captive cattle from Spain to the Tuscan Tiber, fathers Aventinus with Rhea on Mount Aventine, discovers Cacus’s theft, breaks open his cave, and kills him. Formerly Evander’s guest, he is invoked by Pallas before combat with Turnus.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

2 sections; each heading opens the text

And now the mighty labour is begun

Hercules is identified as Aventinus’s father. After killing the triple Geryon, he drove his captive herds from Spain to the banks of the Tuscan Tiber, where he found the priestess Rhea on Mount Aventine and fathered Aventinus.

BOOK VIII

Hercules arrives after killing Geryon and drives his cattle near the Tiber. He discovers Cacus’s theft, breaks open the cave, and kills the monster.

Hercules in Heroides and Amores

Ovid · about 24 to 15 BC · the work’s page · read it where Hercules first appears

Abstract written by a model

Hercules is a hero, son of Jupiter and husband of Deianira, whose long labours arise from Juno’s hostility. He conquers Oechalia, protects earth, bears the Nemean lion, supports Atlas, and is subjugated by Omphale. After conquering Oechalia, he is said to submit to Iole; Deianira hears he is dying from a poisoned cloak.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

1 section; each heading opens the text

IX

Hercules has conquered Oechalia but is said to have submitted to Iole. Deianira recalls his many victories and labours, mocks his service to Omphale, and hears that he is dying from the poisoned cloak.

Hercules in The Library

Apollodorus · AD 1 to 200 · the work’s page · read it where Hercules first appears

Abstract written by a model

Hercules, also named Alcides and called a hero, is a figure defined by exceptional combat, service, travel, and eventual immortality. As an infant, Hera sends serpents to kill him; later she drives him mad, and his madness leads him to burn the children he had by Megara. He is married to Megara, gives her to Iolaus, and later marries Hebe. He has a twin, Iphicles, and is recorded as father of several children by Megara, Epicaste, Chalciope, Astyoche, Thespius’s daughters, and others. As punishment for murdering his children, he serves Eurystheus for twelve years. His labours are described as ten, completed in eight years and a month, though another record calls them twelve. They include killing the Nemean lion and Lernaean hydra, using the hydra’s gall on his arrows, bringing the Cerynitian hind and Erymanthian boar to Mycenae, cleansing the cattle-yard of Augeas, shooting the Stymphalian birds, bringing the Cretan bull, the mares of Diomedes, Hippolyte’s belt, and Cerberus, and killing Geryon or driving away Geryon’s kine. He receives aid or equipment from Athena, the Sun, and Zeus, including brazen castanets and a golden goblet for crossing the ocean. Beyond these services, he kills many enemies and monsters, battles centaurs, fights the Amazons at Themiscyra, sides with the gods against the giants, captures Ilium and Oechalia, conquers Thasos, restores Tyndareus, founds Abdera and the Olympian games, rescues Theseus from Hades, and resurrects Alcestis. He is initiated in the Mysteries of Eleusis and serves Omphale as a slave. His later story includes a poisoned robe, torture, burning himself on Mount Oeta, and obtaining immortality; he is eventually reconciled to Hera.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

12 sections; each heading opens the text

Εἰσὶ δὲ οἱ λέγοντες ᾿Αφαρέα μὲν καὶ Λεύκιππον

Heracles killed Hippocoon and his sons. After their deaths, Tyndareus and Icarius returned to Lacedaemon, and Tyndareus took the kingdom.

23 Ὅτι Θησεύς, Πειρίθῳ συνθέμενος Διὸς θυ-

Heracles brought Theseus up from Hades and sent him to Athens.

Erysichthon, son of Cecrops by

Hercules kills Eryx, Eurition, Eurytion the herdsman of Geryon, Eurytus and Cteatus, Eurytus son of Hippocoon, and Eunomus. He performs labours ordered by Eurystheus, including killing the hydra, pursuing the Stymphalian birds, bringing the Cretan bull, the mares of Diomedes, the belt of Hippolyte, the kine of Geryon, and the apples of the Hesperides. He also cleanses and initiates Eumolpus at Eleusis, crosses the ocean in a golden goblet, wounds Hades, and is carried up to heaven.

Hera, daughter of Cronus and

Hera sends serpents to kill the infant Hercules, drives him mad, stirs up the Amazons against him, afflicts the cows of Geryon with a gadfly, and sends storms against him. Hercules is eventually reconciled to Hera.

Herb, magical, which brings dead

Hercules performs numerous feats, including slaying Linus, the Nemean lion, the Lernaean hydra, Geryon, and other enemies; rescuing Alcestis and Prometheus; cleansing the cattle-yard of Augeas; retrieving the belt of Hippolyte, the golden apples of the Hesperides, and Cerberus; and capturing Troy, Elis, Pylus, and Lacedaemon. He serves Eurystheus at Tiryns, is named Alcides, marries Megara and later Hebe, fathers the Heraclids, and is eventually tortured by a poisoned robe before burning himself on Mount Oeta and obtaining immortality.

Jason, son of Aeson, hunts the

Hercules completes ten labours in eight years and a month, including killing the Nemean lion, the Hydra, and the Cerynitian hind, driving away the cows of Geryon, and bringing the man-eating mares of Diomedes to Eurystheus. He fights the Lapiths, campaigns against Lacedaemon, kills Laomedon and Laogoras, and fights in many other places. His madness leads him to burn the children he had by Megara; he also buries Melas and throws Lichas into the sea.

Meleager, son of Oeneus or Ares

Hercules performs numerous labors and exploits in this unit, including killing the Nemean lion, fighting or killing monsters and enemies, capturing the Cretan bull, visiting Hades, and attacking Oechalia. He also participates in the Argonaut expedition, is initiated into the Mysteries of Eleusis, celebrates the Olympian games, and is burned on Mount Oeta.

Omphale, mistress of Hercules,

Hercules was bought as a slave by Omphale, queen of Lydia, and served her. He fought Amyntor at Ormenium, attacked the sons of Minos at Paros, diverted the Peneus into the cattle-yard of Augeas, founded an altar to Pelops at Olympia, and was the father of several sons, including Onesippus, Onites, Patroclus, Palaemon, and others.

Phlegra, Hercules in the battle of

Hercules is said to have fought in the battle of the gods with the giants at Phlegra. The Pillars of Hercules are mentioned, and Hercules is associated with numerous entries throughout the unit, including the capture of Cerberus, the rescue of Theseus but not Pirithous from Hades, and the burial of Pholus.

Ram with golden fleece carries

Hercules effects the resurrection of Alcestis, receives the poisoned robe from Athena, kills the Scironian and Stymphalian foes, and serves Admetus, Eurystheus, and Omphale under different conditions. He captures Ilium with Telamon, gives Sterope a lock of the Gorgon's hair, heals or guides various heroes, and performs many labours including shooting the Stymphalian birds and crossing the sea in the Sun's golden goblet.

Tereis, mother of Megapenthes by

Hercules conquers or attacks numerous places and peoples, including Thasos, the Thracians, the Thesprotians, and the Amazons. He also rescues Hesione, fights at Themiscyra, takes the tripod from Delphi, and is associated with many children by different women.

Vine discovered by Dionysus,

Hercules sacrificed to Zeus Saviour and was rescued by Zeus when wounded. He killed or was involved in the deaths of various figures and monsters, including the vixen and dog in one account, and Zeus parted him from Apollo.

Abstract written by a model

Hercules, the Tirynthian hero, is suckled by Juno and named as Zeus/Jupiter’s son. He slays Hydra, takes Cos’s chief city, drags and chains Cerberus from Tartarus, and dies on a ridge of Œta.

Hercules in The Metamorphoses of Ovid, Books VIII-XV

Ovid · about AD 8 · the work’s page · read it where Hercules first appears

Abstract written by a model

Hercules is a hero, offspring of Alcmena and Jupiter, though some accounts also make him the putative son of Amphitryon and twin brother of Iphiclus. He has a mortal nature from Alcmena and an immortal nature from Jupiter. Juno persecutes him from before his birth, delaying it through Ilithyïa, and he is destined for many toils. He becomes a protector of earth through feats against monsters, robbers, hostile peoples, and tyrants, especially the mighty, numerous labours conventionally called the Twelve Labours. His exploits include wearing the Nemean lion’s skin; destroying the Hydra of Lerna; cleansing Augeas’s stables; chaining Cerberus; recovering Alcestis; delivering Theseus and Prometheus; defeating the Amazons, Geryon, Antæus, Cacus, Centaurs, and Periclymenus; and killing Laomedon. He rescues Hesione from a sea monster, then sacks Troy when Laomedon refuses the promised horses. He also destroys Messene, Elis, and Pylos, possesses oxen of Iberia, and uses bow and arrows, including against Nessus. He competes with Acheloüs for Deïanira, defeats him despite his serpent and bull forms, breaks off his horn, and is said to regulate the river Acheloüs. After marrying Deïanira, he later loves Iole and has a son, Hyllus; he is also father of Tlepolemus. His end follows Nessus’s attempted seizure of Deïanira. Hercules kills Nessus, but Nessus’s tunic later inflicts unbearable suffering on him. Recalling his labours, Hercules throws Lichas into the sea and lies on a funeral pile on Œta. When his body is consumed, Jupiter receives his immortal part into the heavens. He is added to the heavens, assumed into Olympus, worshipped as a demigod, and marries Youth. Interpretations also identify him as a personification of the Sun or as the heroic ideal of human perfection.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

7 sections; each heading opens the text

BOOK THE NINTH.

Hercules competes with Acheloüs for Deïanira, boasting of Jupiter as his father and of his completed labors. He defeats Acheloüs in physical combat, overcomes his serpent and bull forms, and breaks off his horn. Hercules is later said to have regulated the river Acheloüs by raising banks and uniting its branches into one channel.

FABLE II. [IX.101-272]

Hercules returns victorious with Deïanira, crosses the swollen Evenus by swimming, and kills Nessus when the Centaur attempts to carry her off. After the tunic of Nessus causes him terrible suffering, he recalls his labours, throws Lichas into the sea, and lies upon a funeral pile on Œta. Jupiter receives the immortal part of Hercules into the heavens.

FABLE III. [IX.273-323]

Hercules is the son of Alcmena and Jupiter and is destined for many toils. His birth is delayed by Ilithyïa at Juno’s request, but he is ultimately born when Galanthis deceives the Goddess.

EXPLANATION.

Hercules was presented as the son of Jupiter and Alcmena, or in some accounts as one of twins with Iphiclus, the son of Amphitryon. He performed numerous feats against monsters, robbers, hostile peoples, and oppressive rulers, including the labours assigned by Eurystheus, and traditions eventually made him a divine figure who was received into Olympus.

FABLE IV. [XI.194-220]

Hercules delivers Hesione from the sea monster and demands the horses Laomedon promised him. When Laomedon refuses the reward, Hercules captures and plunders Troy.

FABLES V. AND VI. [XII.536-628]

Hercules overthrows the walls of Messene and destroys Elis and Pylos. In battle with Periclymenus, he shoots the transformed eagle with an unerring arrow and kills him.

BOOK THE FIFTEENTH.

Hercules warns Myscelos in dreams to leave Argos and go to the Æsar, threatening him if he refuses. He then causes the black pebbles cast for Myscelos's condemnation to become white, securing his acquittal.

Hercules in The Library

Apollodorus · about AD 70 to 200 · the work’s page · read it where Hercules first appears

Abstract written by a model

Hercules is a mortal hero, son of Zeus and descendant of Perseus, whose divine parentage is visibly apparent. He is brother to Iphicles, trained in chariot-driving, wrestling, archery, and fencing, armed with divine gifts, a self-cut club, and arrows dipped in hydra gall. He marries Megara and has children by her, but after killing his children in madness he is purified and compelled to serve Eurystheus at Tiryns for twelve years. There he performs the labours: killing the Nemean lion and hydra, capturing beasts, cleansing Augeas’s yard, defeating enemies including Hippolyte and Geryon, obtaining the golden apples, and bringing Cerberus from Hades without weapons. He is purified and initiated at Eleusis before this descent, then returns Cerberus and gives Megara to Iolaus. His career also includes aiding Zeus against Giants and Typhon, releasing Prometheus, ravaging Pylus, restoring Alcestis according to one account, and being left in Mysia while seeking Hylas. After killing Iphitus in madness, he is purified and sold to Omphale for three years, during which he kills Syleus, buries Icarus, and is said to join the voyage to Colchis and the Calydonian hunt. He later leads an army against Troy, captures it, kills Laomedon and his sons except Podarces, and gives Hesione to Telamon. He marries Deianira after defeating Achelous, campaigns and captures cities, then accepts exile after accidentally killing Eunomus. At Trachis he defeats enemies and captures Oechalia, but Deianira’s treatment of his tunic with Nessus’s blood and the hydra’s poison destroys his body. He builds a pyre on Oeta, gives his bow to Poeas, and is carried in a cloud with thunder to heaven, where he becomes immortal and marries Hebe.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

21 sections; each heading opens the text

VI. Περὶ μὲν οὖν Δήμητρος ταῦτα λέγεται"

Heracles is summoned by Athena to aid the gods against the Giants. He shoots Alcyoneus, drags him outside Pallene so that he can die, kills Porphyrion after Zeus strikes him with a thunderbolt, and helps destroy the other Giants by shooting them with arrows. He later joins Zeus in the battle against Typhon by helping restore Zeus’s sinews.

VII. Προμηθεὺς δὲ ἐξ ὕδατος καὶ γῆς ἀνθρώ-

Hercules later releases Prometheus from his bonds on Mount Caucasus.

Now Tyro, daughter of Salmoneus and Alcidice,

Heracles ravaged Pylus and killed Neleus’s sons, including Periclymenus, while Nestor alone was saved.

Πελίον θυγατέρα Αλκηστιν. ἐκείνου 1 δὲ δώσειν

Hercules is said to have fought Hades and brought Alcestis back to Admetus. During the voyage of the Argo, he was left behind in Mysia while searching for Hylas; one account instead says he did not sail then, another that he was left at Aphetae, and another that he reached Colchis and became a leader of the Argonauts.

THE LIBRARY, II. tv. 7-8

Hercules receives weapons and equipment from several gods and uses a club that he cut himself at Nemea. After killing his children in a fit of madness, he is purified by Thespius, goes to Delphi, and is directed to serve Eurystheus at Tiryns for twelve years while performing ten labours.

V. When Hercules heard that, he went to Tiryns

Hercules obeys Eurystheus and performs the labours assigned to him. He kills the Nemean lion, defeats the Lernaean hydra with Iolaus’s help, captures the Cerynitian hind alive, captures the Erymanthian boar alive, and is ordered to remove the dung of the Augean cattle in one day.

THE LIBRARY, II. v. 4-5

Hercules diverts the Alpheus and Peneus into Augeas's cattle-yard and cleanses it in a single day. He drives away the Stymphalian birds, brings the Cretan Bull to Eurystheus, captures Diomedes's mares, and later sails to obtain Hippolyte's belt. At Themiscyra he kills Hippolyte after suspecting treachery, fights the Amazons, and then touches at Troy.

THE LIBRARY, II. v. 9

Hercules promised to rescue Hesione if he received the horses that Zeus had given to Tros. He killed the sea monster and saved Hesione, then threatened to wage war on Troy when Laomedon refused to pay him.

THE LIBRARY, II. v. 9

Hercules is ordered to fetch the red kine of Geryon. He travels to Erythia, kills Orthus, Eurytion, and Geryon, crosses the ocean in a golden goblet, recovers an escaped bull from Eryx, and eventually brings the kine to Eurystheus.

When the labours had been performed in eight

After completing his labours in eight years and a month, Hercules was ordered to undertake an eleventh labour: fetching the golden apples from the Hesperides. While traveling to the apples, he reached the river Echedorus, where Cycnus challenged him to single combat.

THE LIBRARY, 11. v. 11

Hercules learns from Nereus where the golden apples and the Hesperides are, defeats Antaeus, kills Busiris and his son, and rescues Prometheus. He obtains the apples through Atlas and gives them to Eurystheus.

12

Hercules was assigned the twelfth labor of bringing Cerberus from Hades. Before undertaking it, he went to Eumolpus at Eleusis for initiation into the Eleusinian Mysteries and was purified from the killing of the Centaurs.

THE LIBRARY, IIT. v. r1-12

Hercules descends into Hades through Taenarum to fetch Cerberus. He raises Theseus but cannot bring up Pirithous, wrestles Menoetes, obtains Cerberus from Pluto without using his weapons, brings the beast to Eurystheus, and returns it to Hades. Afterward, he gives Megara to Iolaus, fails to obtain Iole from Eurytus, kills Iphitus in a fit of madness, and is sold to Omphale for three years to remedy his disease and pay compensation for the murder. During his servitude, he seizes the Cercopes, kills Syleus and Xenodice, buries Icarus, and is said to take part in the voyage to Colchis and the Calydonian hunt.

Μετὰ δὲ τὴν λατρείαν ἀπαλλαγεὶς τῆς νόσου

Heracles sailed to Ilium with eighteen penteconters and an army of distinguished men who willingly wanted to campaign. He besieged Laomedon, entered the city after Telamon, captured the city, shot Laomedon and his sons except Podarces, and awarded Hesione to Telamon.

THE LIBRARY, II. vr. 3-4

Hercules captures Troy, takes Cos by night, joins the gods against the Giants at Phlegra, conquers Elis and Pylus, and defeats Hippocoon and his sons. He kills Augeas, restores Phyleus to the throne, establishes Olympic rites, and later restores Tyndareus to the kingdom of Lacedaemon. He unknowingly fathers Telephus with Auge and marries Deianira after defeating Achelous.

THE LIBRARY, II. vis. 4-5

Hercules came to Calydon and wooed Deianira, defeating Achelous in the struggle for her hand by breaking off one of his horns. He later campaigned with the Calydonians against Thesprotous, captured a city ruled by Phylas, and fathered Tlepolemus by Astyoche. While staying among the Thesprotous, he sent sons to Thebes and Sardinia for colonization.

THE LIBRARY, II. vir. 6

Hercules marched with the Calydonians against the Thesprotians and took the city of Ephyra. After accidentally killing Eunomus while feasting with Oeneus, he chose to undergo exile and set out toward Trachis with Deianeira.

THE LIBRARY, II. vu. 6-7

Hercules travels to Trachis with Deianira, defeats the Dryopes, aids Aegimius against the Lapiths, kills Cycnus and Amyntor, and captures Oechalia from Eurytus. After Deianira treats his tunic with Nessus's blood, the Hydra's poison corrodes his body; he is carried to Trachis, orders a pyre built on Mount Oeta, and becomes immortal after his death.

THE LIBRARY, IIL. vir. 7

Hercules went to Oeta in the Trachinian territory and constructed a pyre. While the pyre burned, a cloud carried him to heaven with a peal of thunder, after which he obtained immortality, married Hebe, and had numerous sons by Hebe, the daughters of Thespius, and other women.

THE LIBRARY, II. vir. 2

Hyllus acts according to Hercules's commands by marrying Iole and seeking the return of the Heraclids.

IX. ᾿Αρκάδος δὲ καὶ Λεανείρας τῆς ᾿Αμύκλου

Heracles seduced Auge, who consequently bore a baby and hid it in Athena's precinct.

Hercules in Description of Greece

Pausanias · about AD 155 to 175 · the work’s page · read it where Hercules first appears

Abstract written by a model

Hercules is a Greek hero whose identity is also treated as divine: at Sicyon he received sacrifice first as a hero and later as a god, while the Thasians called him a god. His importance lies in the many feats, conflicts, Labours, cults, statues, temples, games, and artistic representations attached to him across Greece. Tradition begins with him throttling snakes, then attributes to him the defeat of the Nemean lion, the shooting of the hydra, and arrows dipped in the hydra’s gall. He released Prometheus after killing the eagle tormenting him, bore or offered to bear Atlas’s burden, fought Geryon, Centaurs, Amazons, and at Pylos, where Athene cooperated with him and Pluto opposed him; he also killed the robber Saurus and destroyed Pylos. After killing Iphitus, he fought Hippocoon and his sons because they would not clear him of guilt and because Œonus was killed. Returning with allies, he defeated them and founded temples to Athene the Exactor of due punishment and Hera Goateater. He defeated the Dryopes and captured their town, cleared Augeas’s land by diverting the Menius, and, after being denied payment, captured and sacked Elis with Argive, Theban, and Arcadian assistance. At Olympia he is linked to an altar and the title Assistant, victories in wrestling and the pancratium, sacrifices to Zeus, the introduction of white poplar, and reliefs of his Labours. He was represented in paintings, on the chest of Cypselus, in votive offerings, and in statues holding club and bow or armed. Traditions place Cerberus’s emergence through a hole at Clymenus’ place, and associate him with temples and images at Sparta, Sicyon, Elis, Thasos, and elsewhere. He also appears as Timosthenes in connection with a Thasian priesthood.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

12 sections; each heading opens the text

CHAPTER VI.

Sicyon had statues and sanctuaries of Hercules, including a stone figure by Scopas and an old wooden image by Laphaes of Phlius. The Sicyonians sacrificed to Hercules both as a hero and, in another part of the festival, as a god.

CHAPTER XXXV.

According to the people of Hermione, Hercules brought Cerberus up through a hole in the ground at Clymenus’ place. He is also said to have dipped his arrows in the gall of the hydra.

CHAPTER XV.

Hercules fought Hippocoon and his sons after they refused to clear him of guilt following the killing of Iphitus and after their sons killed Œonus, Hercules’ nephew. He later returned with allies, defeated Hippocoon and his sons, and built a temple to Athene as the Exactor of due punishment. He is represented armed in a Spartan statue, and traditions also say that he first built a temple to Hera Goateater and sacrificed goats to her during his fight with Hippocoon.

CHAPTER XXXIV.

Hercules defeated the Dryopes when Phylas was king and captured their town on Mount Parnassus. The people of Asine say that they fled after the capture and later crossed to the Peloponnese, where Eurystheus gave them Asine in Argolis.

BOOK V.--ELIS.

Hercules cleared Augeas’s land by diverting the river Menius but was denied his reward. He later defeated Augeas, captured and sacked Elis, killed the sons of Actor at Cleonæ, and persuaded the Eleans to sacrifice to him after they had regarded him as hostile.

CHAPTER VIII.

Hercules is associated with the early games at Olympia, where Clymenus erected an altar to him and gave him the title of Assistant. Hercules is said to have won wrestling and the pancratium, and the temple at Olympia displays many of his Labours.

CHAPTER XI.

Hercules appears in the paintings on the panels around Zeus's throne, including scenes in which he bears the burden of Atlas, fights the Nemean lion, releases Prometheus, and fights the Amazons. Hercules is also said to have dedicated the grove of Pelops, sacrificed to Zeus at Olympia, introduced the white poplar from Thesprotia, and driven flies from the sacrifices by worshipping Zeus the Averter of Flies.

CHAPTER XV.

Hercules appears among the scenes on the chest of Cypselus, seated with his wife during the games in memory of Pelias. He is also shown shooting the hydra that infested the river Amymone.

CHAPTER XVIII.

Hercules is identified on the chest as the armed man approaching Atlas, and he is also shown fighting Geryon and shooting Centaurs. These scenes represent feats attributed to him.

CHAPTER XXII.

Hercules appears in several votive offerings at Olympia. These include Hercules shooting at the Nemean lion, Hercules fighting an Amazon on horseback for her belt, and a ten-cubit brazen Hercules dedicated by the Thasians.

CHAPTER V.

Hercules is said to have defeated the Nemean lion. Polydamas slew a lion on Mount Olympus in an attempt to emulate Hercules.

CHAPTER XXI.

Hercules is associated with several places in the unit: he killed the robber Saurus, had a temple near Saurus's tomb, and is said to have exercised in the Colonnade at Elis. He also destroyed Pylos in Elis, cooperated with Athene in an attack on Pylos, and was opposed there by Pluto.

Hercules in Pausanias' Description of Greece, Volume II

Pausanias · about AD 155 to 175 · the work’s page · read it where Hercules first appears

Abstract written by a model

Hercules is a figure presented through local traditions, monuments, temples, statues, and conflicting accounts, chiefly as the son of Amphitryon who grew up at Thebes. As a boy he killed Linus; later the Thebans say he remitted their yearly tax, mutilated envoys from Orchomenus, fought beside them against the Orchomenians, defeated Erginus, and dedicated a stone lion. They also attribute to him madness, sons by Megara, an attempted attack on Amphitryon, and works redirecting the Cephisus and blocking its passage to the sea. Expelled from Tiryns by Eurystheus, he may have lived at Pheneus with Laonome, where he dug pits and a canal for the Olbius. After capturing Elis, he was said to build the temple of Pythian Apollo; he rode Arion in war against Elis. At Eurystheus's orders he hunted the Erymanthian boar, and traditions differ on whether he killed the Stymphelian birds with arrows or drove them away with rattles. He fathered Echephron and Promachus by Psophis, and a Phigalian story has him rescue Phialo and her exposed boy. He received Dexamenus's hospitality, loved Sostratus and erected his tomb, and is associated with a cave and small statue at the Buraicus. After killing Iphitus, he fought Apollo over and carried off a tripod before returning it. Accounts also distinguish him from Egyptian Hercules, question whether he founded an ancient temple, and identify another Hercules as an Idæan Dactyli.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

11 sections; each heading opens the text

CHAPTER XII.

According to the tradition of the Phigalians, Hercules had an intrigue with Phialo, the daughter of Alcimedon. Hearing a jay imitate the exposed boy's cries, Hercules found Phialo, loosed her from her bonds, and saved the boy's life.

CHAPTER XIV.

Hercules was said to have lived at Pheneus with Laonome, the mother of Amphitryon, and may have gone there after being expelled from Tiryns by Eurystheus. He dug pits at the mountains Oryxis and Sciathis and a channel through the plain for the river Olbius, though the river later returned to its old channel.

CHAPTER XV.

Hercules is said by the people of Pheneus to have built the temple of Pythian Apollo after capturing Elis. Heroes who joined his expedition against Elis are said to have been killed in the battle and buried near the temple.

CHAPTER XXII.

Hercules was said to have dealt with the man-eating birds of Stymphelus. One tradition says that he killed them with arrows, while Pisander of Camirus said that he frightened them away with rattles.

CHAPTER XXIV.

Hercules fathered Echephron and Promachus by Psophis. At the orders of Eurystheus, he hunted the Erymanthian boar on the banks of the Erymanthus.

CHAPTER XI.

The Thebans show the monument of Hercules’ sons by Megara and say that Hercules, in madness, also wished to kill Amphitryon. He is honored in a temple called Champion, near a gymnasium and racecourse named after him, and colossal statues of Hercules and Athene were offered there by Thrasybulus and the Athenians.

CHAPTER XVII.

Hercules was preparing to fight the Orchomenians with the Thebans when an oracle required the voluntary death of one of the most notable citizens for victory. He dedicated a stone lion before the temple of Artemis Euclea after defeating the Orchomenians and Erginus.

CHAPTER XXVII.

One tradition says that Hercules slept with fifty daughters of Thestius in a single night, sparing one and making her the perpetual virgin priestess of his temple at Thespia. Another says that he slept with all of them and fathered sons, but the account rejects this as incredible and doubts that Hercules son of Amphitryon founded the ancient temple, suggesting instead Hercules of the Idæan Dactyli.

CHAPTER XXXVII.

When Hercules grew up at Thebes, he had the yearly tax imposed on the Thebans remitted. The Minyæ then met with great reverses in the war, leading Erginus to make peace with him.

CHAPTER XXXVIII.

Hercules has a temple and small statue about seven stades from Orchomenus. The Thebans say that he diverted the river Cephisus into the Orchomenian plain and dammed its passage to the sea under the mountain.

CHAPTER XIII.

Hercules appeared in a group at Delphi fighting Apollo for possession of a tripod, with Athene attempting to calm him. When Hercules the son of Amphitryon consulted the oracle after killing Iphitus, Xenoclea initially refused to answer him; he seized and carried away the tripod, then returned it after she identified him as a different Hercules from the Egyptian Hercules.

Hercules in The Fall of Troy

Quintus Smyrnaeus · about AD 200 to 299 · the work’s page · read it where Hercules first appears

Abstract written by a model

Hercules, a mighty glorious hero, fathers Telephus and becomes Eurypylus’s grandfather, whose strength and shield honor him.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

1 section; each heading opens the text

BOOK VI

Hercules is identified as Eurypylus's grandfather. Eurypylus bears a shield depicting Hercules' great labours, and Zeus grants Eurypylus extraordinary strength as a favour to Hercules.

Hercules in Bulfinch's Mythology: The Age of Fable

Thomas Bulfinch · 1855 · the work’s page · read it where Hercules first appears

Abstract written by a model

Hercules is a hero, son of Jupiter and Alcmene, cousin and subject of Eurystheus through Juno’s arts. Renowned among the Argonauts, he leaves them at Mysia while seeking Hylas. He performs the twelve Labors, including strangling the Nemean lion, cleansing the Augean stables, gaining Hippolyta’s girdle and the golden apples, defeating Antaeus and Cacus, and bringing Cerberus from Hades and returning him. He captures Pygmies, has fatal arrows needed for Troy, and serves Omphale for three years. As Dejanira’s suitor, he defeats Achelous and rescues Alcestis from Death; he later marries Dejanira. Poisoned by Nessus’s blood, he is consumed on Mount Oeta, becomes immortal, and Jupiter takes him to heaven, where he dwells among the stars.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

3 sections; each heading opens the text

Chapter XI

Hercules was one of the renowned heroes among the Argonauts. He left the expedition at Mysia while searching for Hylas, who had been taken by the nymphs of a spring. Some accounts say Hercules later went overland to Colchis, where he performed many mighty deeds.

Chapter XII

Hercules was the son of Jupiter and Alcmena, and Juno opposed him from birth. He performed the twelve Labors of Hercules, defeated numerous monsters and giants, and was eventually poisoned by a robe treated with the blood of Nessus. After his mortal part was consumed on Mount Oeta, Jupiter took his immortal nature to heaven, where he married Hebe.

Chapter XVI

Hercules competed with Achelous for Dejanira and defeated him first in wrestling, then in his serpent and bull forms. He also rescued Alcestis by seizing Death and forcing him to release her.

Heracles in An Astronomer's Wife

Angelo Hall · 1908 · the work’s page · read it where Heracles first appears

Abstract written by a model

Heracles, presented in Angeline Hall’s poem as the genius of labor, performs courageous, enduring tasks to please God, bless humanity, and practice virtue: he rescues Hesione, overcomes Death, receives Helios’s golden boat, and models demanding work.

Narrative, section by section written by a model

1 section; each heading opens the text

------------------------------------------------------------------------ CHAPTER XV.

Heracles is presented in Angeline Hall’s poem as the genius of labor, performing difficult tasks not as a base slave but to please God, bless humanity, and practice virtue. His labors, courage, and endurance serve as a fitting image of Angeline Hall’s own demanding work.
